OFFICE OF STRATEGIC SERVICES

RESEARCH AND ANALYSIS BRANCH
29 JUNE 1945
THE JAPANESE PRESS AS AN INSTRUMENT FOR CONTROL
The Japanese press is a powerful instrument for social and political
control. Through its assumption of a role in the lives of the Japanese
people in which it not only guided their knowledge of world events but
also molded their cultural tastes and in part directed their social affairs,
the press in Japan has exerted strong influence over the population. To
894.91/6-2945
exploit this instrument of control to the utmost, the Japanese Govern-
ment, particularly since 1942, has centralized both newspaper manage-
ment and government supervision.
Through the combination of extremely wide circulation, broad range
of interests, and modern, efficient methods of publication, the Japanese
newspaper achieves a far-reaching and intimate influence upon the
Japanese people. Enormous circulations are made possible in part by
the fact that nearly 99 percent of the people can read newspapers and
magazines. The range of the Japanese newspaper's interest extends not
only to printing issues in Braille and publishing simplified editions for
children but also to matters completely outside the realm of publication.
Each of the large daily newspapers, through its "enterprise" bureau,
has undertaken such projects as welfare work, scientific expeditions,
meteorological observations, agricultural experiments, and aeronautical
demonstrations. In its methods of obtaining, transmitting, publishing,
and distributing news the Japanese newspaper is nearly as efficient as
its counterpart in the United States.
The Japanese press has not shown an equivalent zeal for accuracy
in presenting the news. When complete facts are missing, reporters
and desk men round out their reports with whatever fiction will appeal
to their readers, and they also try to meet their readers'demand for
rumor and gossip. Together with the absence of national libel laws,
this disregard for accuracy has established a tradition of yellow journa-
lism which has made the Japanese press especially pliable to the war-
time demands of the Japanese Government. Newspapermen have not
been reluctant to print long, detailed accounts of nonexistent naval
battles or to treat factually the propaganda themes their Government
has evolved.
894.91
The measures taken by the Japanese Government to secure control
of this efficient and adaptable medium have been of three kinds: negative
censorship, centralization of newspapers, and centralization of the
sources of news.
The first of these controls has always been a part of Japanese journal-
ism. The original press laws antedate the Japanese Constitution of 1889.
/6.2945

=== Page 2 === (confidence 0.99)
GONFIDENTIAL
The press has also long been restrained by the peace preservation and
thought control measures which provide that no treasonable or commu-
nistic ideas may be presented, and that nothing may be said which
might alienate the people from the military. Wartime control legislation
has been considerably more restrictive, but the principle of telling the
press what must not be printed instead of what must be printed has
been retained. Although there has been a tacit understanding since
1937 that militarism and nationalism should be made attractive, the
Japanese press has not been obliged to disseminate any particular ideol-
ogy, and within the limits of wartime security has been relatively free
to criticize government policy.
To implement this negative censorship the Japanese Government
has set up a system of licensing, press bans, confiscation and suspension
of publications, and fines and imprisonment for editors. Most of these
restraints are at present administered by the Bureau of Information
which also directs general press policy. For printing information pro-
hibited by a news ban, a Japanese publisher faces, in addition to the
revocation of his license, the possibility of confiscation of the offending
issues, the complete suspension of all his publications in addition to his
newspaper, or the fining and jailing of his editor. Furthermore, he may
be forced to forfeit the 2,000 yen of guarantee money that all publications
dealing with current events are obliged to post. Because of these severe
penalties, Japanese newspapermen frequently practice voluntary pre-
publication censorship, submitting material to the Bureau of Informa-
tion for approval in advance of its publication.
Although press bans may be issued on any piece of information
the Japanese Government wishes to withhold, the material most fre-
quently banned is that which is termed "inimical to public peace and
order." Bans are also placed on material which is thought to fall into
one of the following four categories: information that may obstruct
foreign diplomatic relations; information that may threaten national
security; information that may obstruct the carrying out of economic
policies; information that may threaten the security of military affairs.
Bans may be issued by the Home Ministry, the War Ministry, the Navy
Ministry, and the Foreign Ministry, and are generally released over the
signature of the local police chief.
The Japanese Government began to centralize newspaper produc-
tion shortly after war began in China in 1937. Between 1937 and 1943
the number of daily newspapers was reduced from over 1,000 to less than
200. Furthermore, in 1942 the large metropolitan newspapers were forced
by government decree to merge with one another, reducing the number
of daily newspapers in Tokyo to one-fourth of the prewar number. Many
of the smaller provincial newspapers went out of business at that time
through shortage of newsprint, and their communities were then served
by the larger newspapers. Although the newsprint shortage was an-
nounced as the main reason for the forced merger, the tightening of
government supervision was clearly a desired result. In line with this
2
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.99)
a
CONFIDENTIAL
policy of increased centralization, the Japan Press Association was estab-
lished in 1942 as a government control organization, and early in 1945
was succeeded by the Japan Press Corporation. It supervises the registra-
tion of newspapermen, the distribution of newspapers and other publi-
cations, and the allotment of newsprint and other materials.
The centralization of news sources was largely achieved through the
forced merger in 1936 of the two leading news agencies, Rengo and Nip-
pon Dempo, to form Domei. The formation of this monopoly increased
the inaccuracy traditional to Japanese journalism. Newspapers had pre-
viously subscribed to both the leading news services and had printed
conflicting versions of the same event side by side, thus allowing readers
to draw their own conclusions. With Domei as the only Japanese news
service, even this check on accuracy was removed. Some Japanese news-
papers, resisting this action by the Government, sought to avoid using
Domei by relying to a greater extent upon their own correspondents and
by establishing agreements with the American news services and the
New York Times and the London Times. Since 1942, of course, these
agreements have been abrogated. Except for a handful of foreign corres-
pondents Domei has become, as the Japanese Government intended,
virtually the only source of outside news. This centralization of news
sources, along with the establishment of controls over subject matter
and the centralization of newspaper production, completed the shaping
of an instrument of social and political control which, whether used by
the Japanese Government in prosecuting the war or by an Allied Military
Government in restoring peacetime order, has few equals in compre-
hensiveness or efficiency.

Gentlemen:
I am attempting to collect enough data on the Press of Japan
since the Occupation to be able to do a comprehensive paper on the
differences or similarities between prewar and postwar newspapers
and publications of that country.
I would greatly appreciate a list of any or all publications
available in your section dealing with the subject of the Press of
Japan since the Occupation and during the war years.
A more detailed description of the type of information for
which I am seeking is as follows:
1. General MacArthur's Press Code for Japan.
2.
General MacArthur's monthly announcements concerning Japan,
particularly when the monthly announcement has oncerned
the Press of Japan.
P
二
3.
Number of newspapers operating in Japan today, t8tay;
CS/A
number of newspapers operating in Tokyo today, totat;
and the number of English-language newsapers opesating
in Japan today, total.
4.
Details of the production strikes of the Yomiuri Shimbun
and the Hokkaido Shimbun which were investigated by the
CI&E section of SCAP.
5.
Some indication of the political affiliation of the more
important Japanese newspapers since the new democratic
forms were instigated by SCAP.
6.
As much information as it is permissible for your office
to distribute concerning the activities of the newspaper
unions of Japan, present-day.
7.
Any information that has been made available concerning
the number of English-language newspapers that continued
operation in Japan during the war years (WWII).

=== Page 108 === (confidence 0.98)
FOREWORD
News agencies play a prominent role in the dissemi-
nation of information in Jepan. In addition to the two
large national agencies, Kyodo and Jiji, which resemble
the associated Press and the United Press in the United
States in many respects, there exist in Japan a great
multitude of smaller agencies, some of which cannot be
considered as news agencies in the American sense. Yet
these smaller agencies app or to perform an important
role in providing a specialized informational service to
limitec groups of subscribers, or in providin background
or interpretative material which is not normally pub-
lished in the reduced-size Japanese news apers.
Although a number of reports on individual agencies
have been prepared by the Information Media Teseerch
Unit, Civilian Information and Education Section, Supreme
Commander for the A1lied Powers, since November 1945,
some of which have been published in mimeographed form,
this is the first comprehensive report that has been pre-
pared in the field of news agencies in Jspan.

PRE DOMEI
Before there cen be news agencies, there must be news-
papers. In America, the modern daily newspaper has a his-
tory of more than 15o years1 and the news agency of over
100 years.2 Jepan, in its pre-Admirel Perry isolationism,
was not exposed to the modern development of free and wide-
spreed nevs disseminetion.3 Its feudal rulers apparently
neither could conceive nor imegine the necessity for inform-
ing the masses during the centuries in which Jepan pursued
its lonely course.
However, in l664, not long after Perry opened the
closed door of Japan, during the period immedietely preced-
ing the Meiji Re:toration (1868), the first newspeper,
Shimbunshi, appeered.4 A querter of a century later, news
agencies appeered to gather and distribute news.5 Thus,
the history of modern journelism is only &3 years old in
Japen, and news agencies only 58 years old.
In those early days, cevelopment of both newspapers
and news agencies foreshadowed Domei and the recent wartime
and prewar Jepanese press. They served as mouthpieces of
political factions rather than presenting factual and ob-
jective news.6 News agencies were more edvertising than

A SURVEY OF JAPANESE NEWS AGENCIES
"news" agencies; their news covering was largely collect-
ing and distributing metrcrolitan news for provincial news-
papers.7
Towards the end of the Meij1 Era (1868-1911) and dur-
ing the period of Japan's successful wars against China end
Russia, there emerged two rival8 domestic agencies, Teikoku
(The Teikoku Tsushin Sha) and Dentsu (The Nippon Dempo
Tsushin Sha). Both hed head offices in Tokyo, and brenches
throughout the nation. Their interests did not extend be-
yond the frontiers of Japan, which still lacked free access
to world news channels.
Dentsu, inaugurated in 190l, wes the more important.
It made mild overtures towerd foreign coverage when it sent
a correspondent to the Paris ece Conference and later to
the Weshington Conference (1921).
Politically, Dentsu inclined towards the conservative
Seiyukai (Politicel Friendship Party), Teikoku towerds the
liberal Kenseikai (Constitutional Government Party).9
Lack of a  Japanese-controlled means to convey Japan's
news to the rest of the world and to accumulate interna-
tional news caused growing concern among Jepanese newspeper-
men.10 In the first decade of the present century, the in-
ternational news field was divided by contractual arrenge-
ments among Reuters of Great Britain, Havas of France, Wolf
of Germany, Stefani of Italy, Uestnik of Russia, and Asso-
ciated Press of America, etc.ll The pivotal position was
occupied by Reuters, lergely because most of the world's
cables were under British control, and trensmission of news
depended upon cables. Japan, in the glcbe's far eestern
corner, was entirely dependent for world news upon Reuters!
omnibus service to the Far East, which was dropped all along.
the way from London at Athens, Singapore, Hong Kong, and

Shanghei, and thence wes partially relayed to Tokyo.12
Japan's increased steture in the world, due in part
to its successful wars witi Russia and China, as well as
its increasing intercourse with-the rest of the world,
encouraged newsmen in Jepan to think of forming an inter-
national press service.13
Anti-Japanese agitation on the west coast of America
in the early years of the Taisho era (1912-1925) prompted
a roup of public-spirited business men, headed by Vis-
count Yeiichi SHIBUSAWA, to esteblish Kokusai (The Koku-
sai Tsushin Sha) in February 1914 14 Their purpose was,
among other things, to bring about better understanding
between the United Etates and Japan through exchenge of
news.15 Kokusai acguired contrects with Reuters and the
Associated Press.16 J, R. Kennedy, who for many years
had been Tokyo correspordent for the AP, was made general
maneger, with virtually dictatorial powers.17
The establishing of Kokusai the very year World War
I broke out was timely. It meant that Japan could have
its own coverege of the historic conflict. Kokusai's
original purpose of fostering American-Japanese under-
standing became secondary in the light of the greater and
more dramatic events then taking place.18 Prompted by
urgent requirements for wer news, Kokusai opened up new
routes of news service direct to Tokyo from New York,
London, Paris, Petrograd, Rome, and other importent world
centers, thus bringing Japan into closer and direct con-
tect with the world's leeding powers.19
World War I, thereforc, marked a new epoch in the
history of Japanese news agencies by bringing ebout im-
portant changes in both their domestic and internetional

development.20 In the domestic field, the two competing
news and advertising agencies, Teikoku and Dentsu, were
obliged to handle not only donestic but also foreign news.
in order to meet the demends of their provinciel clients.
Teikoku made arrengements with Reuters and the AP, through
Kokusai; Dentsu mede similer arrangements with the UP of
America.21 In the international news field, Kokusai, under
Kennedy's management, was dominent.22
Another development was the establishment of Toho
(The Toha Tsushin Sha), orgenized in 1917 by Japanese resi-
dents in China with the finencial support of the Japenese
Foreign Office.23 It began opereting in 1920. The ori-
ginal object of Toho wes to bring Jepanese news into China
to compete with Reuters of Great Britain, Havas of France,
Trensoceen of Germany, Roste and later TAss of Russia, and
the UP and INS of America.?4 A few yeers later, Toho moved
from Shanghai to Tokyo ana pegan to supply Chinese news to
It soon extended its service to handling news from Russia,
for which it gained en excellent reputation.26

stormy, troubled existence.It suffered from the world-

DOMEI
Incorporated in 1935 under the Public Service Asso-
ciation Articles of the Civil Law, Domei (The Domei Tsus-
hin Sha) for 10 years was the dominating colossus of the
news agency field, the force which welded together the
press of a totalitarian Japan during what were probably
the 10 most eventful years in Japanese history.
The establishment of omei solved several problems,
if problems they were. It ended the cut-throat rivalry
between Rengo and Dentsu. It also ended the ruthless
competition between metropolitan and provincial papers
and the long controversy wnich had developed between the
newspapers and the Japan Broadcasting Corporation over
news broadcasting.1
Domei became the all-poverful means by which news
was dispensed. On 1 July 1941 Dome1 had 141 member pa-
pers, including practically every daily newspaper in
Japan.2 Also a member was the Japan Broadcasting Cor-
poration.3
Domei's objective was the collection and distribu-
tion of news, both fore gn and domestic.4 It exchanged
news with ali the provinciel papers throughout the coun-
try es well as with all representative nationel press
agencies of the I ading powers, including the AP, the UP,
Reuters, Havas, TASS, and DNB.5 Maintaining the prinsi-
ple of free competition in collection of news, Domei com-
peted freely with the leading Osaka and Tokyo papers in
both domestic and foreign fields. In the distribution
of news its facilities were open to all daily papers

KYODO TSUSHIN SHA
Born of Japan's defeat in the war, Kyodo is Japenese
newspaperdom's effort to tie in the physical assets end
experiences of the past with the ideologicel demends that
ceme from being defeated by & democracy. Biggest, broedest,
and most extensive of the numerous egencies that heve ap-
peared or reappeered in Jepan since the Occupetion began,
it is, because of its inheritance from Domei, most closely
tied to the historic pattern. that began with Dentsu end.
Teikoku, extended through Kokusai and Rengo, and ended
with Domei.

Kyodo.71 Inasmuch as Jiji is dependent upon Kyodo for

withdrew his proposel. All agreed thet any chenge would
involve the denger of undermining financially both or-
ganizations with cut-throat competition.

GENERAL DISCUSSION OF NEWS AGENCIES IN JAPAN
Besides the major genealogical line thet began with
Teikoku and Dentsu and fircs its present day representa-
tion in Kyodo and Jiji, the news agency family in Japan
has many other smaller and less importent branches. Only
by the most generous stretch of the imeginetion, however,
could meny of these qualify as news agencies in the Ameri-
can sense.1
Many of these seconctry Japanese news agencies lack
the institutional quality of the AP, UP, Reuters, TASS,
and in Japan, Kyodo. Instead, some are similar to Ameri-
can "information letters," "private informatio services,"
and "tip services"; while others in a sense rese ible such
popular American colunns es Drew Pearson's, Westbrok Peg-
ler's and Louella Parsons'.
hpparently, the ro e of these agencies in providing
a specialized type of information to a limited group of
subscribers is hihly importent in contemporary Jepan.
For instance, sucn agencies as those which specialize in
labor (e.g., Nippon Rono Tsushin), cnd number many trade
union leaders among their clientele, are far more influ-
ential than their circulations indicate. Others, such as
the well-known Okayama Service, provide a translation ser-
vice for correspondents and Occupation authorities.
Except for the highly specialized egencies, the pri-
mary function of nost of the smaller services is to fur-
nish background material, interpretetions, or confiden-
tial information which is not normelly in the press.
Such agencies nay appeal to certain special elements or
interested groups.2 There are those (e.E., Tairiku Joho
Tsushin) which are easily compareble to publicity egen-
1. Information Media Research, News Services, unpublished
report, 6 November 1945, p. 1.
2. Ibid., passim.
33

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
34
A SURVEY OF JAPANESE NEWS AGENCIES
cies in the United States; and others (e.g., Godo Tsushin)
which are similar to American trade journals and fan pub-
lications.
Only in a very limited sense can any of then be clessi-
fied as competitors to Kyodo. But the smallness of these
agencies should not exclude them as important factors in
the contemporary Japanese scene. An agency with a ceuse
to adyocate is a tool to put over its own ideas or the
ideas of the group it reprosents. By hammering eway at an
interested but inactive minority, it may forge leaders and
inspire action.
The fect that tens of thousands of Japanese ere will-
ing to support 50-ocd agencies by paying prices ranging as
high as vi,o00 monthly merely to acquire more information
then their fellow citizens have access to in the deily
press is an interesting phenomenon in itsclf. It is remi-
niscent of pre-Eighteenth Century Europe, where rulers and
other powerful men spent sizecble sums to heve special
couriers bring them news from afar. In pert, the peper
shortage accounts for this. Daily newspapers in Jepan are
limited to two pages each issue. This meens that deteiled
news and special news are generally unavailable except
through news agencies. It also implies that the subscriber
to a news service is a person of influence, i.e., a trede
union leader, politician, educator, banker, Government of-
ficial, industrielist, or such. The informetion he pur-
chases from news agencies may be part of his stock-in-
trade.
Most of the current crop f smaller agencics sprang
up as a result of Japan's defeat, after which limiting the
number of periodicals in each field by arbitrary Govern-
ment action was discontinuec.3 Some of today's egencies
have a prewer history. Of these, a few had been suspended
during the war by the Government, others hed continued
without interruption.
That there are so many of these minor and special-
izing agencies in Japan now does not mean that this is a

RADIOPRESS1
Before it was a news agency, Radiopress was a little-
known but important cog in the machinery of the Jepanese
Foreign Office. During the wer when Japan was fairly well
shut off from access to world news, the Foreign Office em-
ployed approximately 35 American-born Jepanese to monitor
world-wide shortwave broadcasts. This small group, work-
ing on a 24-hour schedule, produced a compact mimeographed
news pamphlet which was circulated among members of the
Foreign Office and the Imperial Family. The covers of
every issue were stamped "strictly secret."
With cessation of hostilities, the Forcign Office was
anxious to continue the service. There was also a wide-
spreed interest on the pert of other Japanese for news from
the outside world. The same Nisei, using equipment borrowed
from the Foreign Office, continued the service as an inde-
pendent news agency when 30 of them formed Radiopress on a

Radiopress releases are published in English and dis-

These subscribers include Japan's leading newspapers, Radio
Tokyo, Kyodo News Agency, Japanese Government and foreign
military mission offices, and important civilian units such

Severel editions are published daily in two categor-

typewritten and goes to subscribers immediately after copy
is cleared by censorship. Ordinary Service is slower be-

costs from ¥3,000 to ¥5,000 per month, depending upon the
size of the subscribing newspaper's circulation. The Or-
dinary Service costs ¥1,Ooo per month for each issue.
Because the two-page Japenese dailies are unable to
cover all important events, Radiopress attempts to comple-
ment their coverage by issuing supplements called Special
Editions. These are published on special events and con-
tain such items as verbatim r ports of Allied press con-
ferences, full-recorded Allied speeches, and full-length
commentaries on current special events.
Radiopress has no stated editorial policy other than
"objective reporting." Its coverage is world-wide. It

station at Frankfurt, Germeny. It elso makes use of

Readers' Digest, and the New York Times' Overseas Edition.

LABOR AGENCIES
To act as spokesman for the postwar union labor move-
ment and to give it direction, several small news agen-
cies either were forred or revived from prewar days, while
other agencies with similar intent readjusted their poli-
cies to meet the new situation. On the political level,
two ere cefinitely leftist; two may be terned liberel,
with the distinction, however, that cne is mildly friend-
ly to the Communists, while the other is strongly anti-
Communist; the remaining two are nonpartisan.
Some of the key person:lities in these prolebor news
agencies, by maintaining an easy "adjustability," meneged
to ride through the var almost unscethed; others, by main-
taining & ciscreet silence and inactivity, preserved their
strength for the future. The most militant ones, on the
other hand, suffered, but finally emerged as the ideolo-
gical spokesmen of nilitant labor groups.

JAGA

OFD - Hubert F. Havlik
SUBJECT:
Charges for Press Wire News Services in Japan
It is understood that in analogy to the procedures
maintained by OMGUS Mr. Draper, Undersecretary of the
Department of the Army, is prepared to recommend that the
press wire news services operating in Japan be required
to pay 25 percent in U.S. dollars and the balance in
Japanese currency of the Japanese share of such services
affecting inward-and outward-bound news. By contrast
SCAP has recommended that l00 percent of such services
be payable in Japanese yen instead of the present arrange-
ments whereby 100 percent of these services are payable
in U.S. dollars.
OFD does not believe that the matter is of great
8
94.91/8
importance to the Department, and in the absence of any
other views is prepared to concur with the action proposed
by the Undersecretary of the Army. It should be clearly
understood that this decision is taken without prejudice
to any possible future policy judgment which the Department
may be called upon to make relative to the compensation of
the press wire news services for the cost or value of news
supplied by them to Japan.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

Subject: Anticipated Reorganization in Japanese Newspaper Field.
8
A 10 10
STATE
ANCH
ThE Honorable
5
TheSecretary of State,
CE
Hashington.
I have the honor to report a number of significant read-
justments which are taking place in Japanese newspaper publishing.
Relations of Kyodo News Agency with Big Newspapers
894.91/1
FE
It is understood that Kyodo News Agency may lose, at a date
so far undetermined, the patronage of Japan's two newspapers of
largest circulation: Mainichi and Asahi. As the Department is
Pos
aware, these two newspapers are published simultaneously daily in
Tokyo, Osaka and Kyushu, the total circulation of each of the two
chains being between three and one half and three and three quarter
SA/M
millions daily. These two newspapers thus print one third of
021
Japan's national daily total of between nineteen and twenty million
copies.
349
Pc/R
It is also understood that Mainichi and Asahi have recently
reviewed their relationship with Kyodo and have found that they
use approximately three per cent of the material supplied them by
Kyodo. This news agency is a service organization, its operational
C
expenses being defrayed by member papers in proportion to circulation.
S
Asahi and Mainichi are therefore in the position of paying between
one third and one half of Kyodo's expenses. The managements of both
DB
these newspapers are understood to feel that their support of Kyodo
in effect finances a service of direct benefit not to themselves
but to their competitors.
Asahi is the principal outlet in Japan for the Associated
EFD 101940
Press and Mainichi is the principal outlet for the United Press.
FILED
Both newspapers buy a number of supplementary services.
Merger of Small Newspapers in Tokyo
The three large metropolitan Tokyo newspapers have in the past
few months been reaching into the afternoon field. Since the
surrender
HESTRICTED

=== Page 195 === (confidence 1.00)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 21,
January 13, 1949.
-2-
surrender, Yomiuri (circulation 1,750,000) has had a hand in the
afternoon field through Shin Hochi Shimbun. A recent merger of
Shin Hochi and Yukan Miyako to form Hochi Shimbun has practically
doubled the availability of rationed paper to this Yomiuri sub-
sidiary.
Asahi has long been known to have a working arrangement with
Tokyo Shimbun and thus through this satellite to be in the afternoon
field.
Mainichi has now caught up with its two largest competitors in
Tokyo in this respect. A combination of financial and personnel
difficulties in Tokyo Mimpo forced it to sell out for a reported
¥7,000,000, of which Mainichi is understood to have supplied nearly
half. Mimpo had no assets worth mentioning except its paper ration.
The Mimpo staff has been dismissed and Mainichi's venture in the
afternoon field, called Tokyo Nichi Nichi, is produced in the Mainichi
editorial rooms and plant.
Shimbun Kyokai Ho, the house organ of the Japan Newspaper Pub-
lishers' and Editors' Association (Nippon Shimbun Kyokai), in a recent
staff article ascribed these mergers and reorganizations to financial
difficulties among the small papers founded after the surrender.
The legality of the organization of Tokyo Nichi Nichi is questioned
by Shimbun No Shimbun ("Newspaper of Newspapers"), a trade paper of
small circulation in Tokyo. Shimbun No Shimbun considers that Mainichi's
reach into the afternoon field constitutes a violation of the Anti-
Monopoly Law.
Respectfully yours,
W. J. Sebald

Enclosure to Despatch No.684 dated

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
FAR EAST COMNAND
Public Information Office
Future Release:
1430
23 September 1949
THIS IS A FUTURE RELEASE
The following statement, given in ADVANCE and in CONFIDENCE, is for release
at 1630 hours 30 September, 1949, Tokyo time:
GENERAL MaCARTHUR'S STATEMENT ON NATTONAL NEISFAI ER JEK IN JAPAN
In the observance of National Newspaper Week in Japan, General MacArthur to-
day issued the following statement:
"In reflecting upon the responsibility of the public press in the building
of the new Japan it is heartening, indeed, to note impressive strides over the
year just past toward a press which is truly free and resolutely objective--free
in the sense that it is not under arbitrary restrictions of the general law nor
subservient to the narrow rigidity of special interests or monopolistic control;
objective in the sense that its publishers, editors and writers honestly seek to
propagate the truth concerninghappenings and events.
"Such progress is fundamental to the building of a society which is free. For
keystone to the arch of representative democracy is a dynamic and informed public
opinion which in turn must draw of its direction from the public press. In modern
society the press is a powerful force for good or for evil.
"For four years I have closely observed the evolution of a responsible Japan-
ese press under the impetus of the vision and courage of many of its leaders and
the avidity with which the people have searched its columns for information and
guidance. During this transition of the Japanese press from the former strictures
of despotic control to its present freedom, with progress linited only by the
patriotism, the integrity, and the fearlessness of those who report the news and
fashion the editorial viowpoint, the progressive marshalling of its full power
and influence behind the forces of law and order and good government has been one
of tho moet encouraging developnents of postwar Jápan..
"In a world still part enslaved and part free, where some of the press is
committed to the advance of freedom and some in opposition--where some seek with
forthrightness and courage to advance the truth for the benefit of all the people
and some to suppress it in furtherance of special privilege or minority rule--the
publishers, editors and writers of Japan, free to chart their own voluntary course:
have, indeed, a challenging opportunity."
-0-

Occasional requests are received here to provide
information on the Japanese press, the latest instance
being a request of the Council on Foreign Relations
for its Folitical Handbook of the World. In addition,
Department would benefit from better information than
currently available on the particular policies and
party affiliations of the various Japanese papers,
which, as pointed out in Mission's confidential
xR
despatch No. 690 (page 3), are not always taken into
consideration by American correspondents.
894.911
894.91/11-1649
Mission is therefore requested to prepare at its
convenience a summary of the policies and affiliations
of leading Japanese ncwspapers, together with an
estimate of their circulation and influence.

=== Page 203 === (confidence 0.98)
OFFICE OF THE UNITED STATES POLITICAL ADVISER
Tokyo, Japan, Desember 15, 1945.
NO. 106
SUBJECT:
Transmission of Japanese Newspapers for
Distribution to Japanese Colonies in
South America.
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the seeond paragraph
of the Department's telegram No. 172, November s0, 7 p.m.,
stating that leaders of the Japanese colony in Brazil
have requested copies of current Japanese newspapers,
and to forward under separate cover a colleetion ef Tokyo
newspapers issued since the surrender.
In connection with the distribution of these mews-
papers in Brazil, it is suggested that part of the
colleotion might be forwarded to the Embassy in Limm,
Peru, in view of the large Japanese population in Peru
and the likelihood that Japanese there may also remain
unconvinced of the fact of defeat and ignorant of con-
ditions in Japan since the surrender. It is believed
that the distribution of such newspapers will have a
salutary propaganda effect upon the Japanese in Latin
America, revealing to them the progress toward demoora-
tization now taking place in their homeland.
Respectfully yours,
George Atoheson, Jr.
Accompaniment:
One package containing
Japanese newspapers.
Copy to Consulate Ceneral, Sao Paolo, Brazil.
Copy to Embassy, Lima, Peru.
Original and duplioate, with reverse
carbon, to Department.
